Public problem:
Find the number of positive integer palindromes written in base $10$ with no zero digits, and whose digits add up to $13$. For example, $42124$ has these properties. Recall that a palindrome is a number whose representation reads the same from left to right as from right to left.
assistantfinish_reason=stop
To find the number of positive integer palindromes in base 10 with no zero digits and a digit sum of 13, we will proceed through the following logical steps:

### 1. Problem Analysis and Constraints

*   **Palindrome Property**: A number reads the same forwards and backwards. This means the sequence of digits is determined entirely by the first $\lceil n/2 \rceil$ digits, where $n$ is the total number of digits.
*   **No Zero Digits**: Every digit $d_i \in \{1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9\}$.
*   **Digit Sum**: The sum of all digits is 13.
*   **Structure**:
    *   If the number of digits $n$ is even ($n=2k$), the digits are $d_1, d_2, \dots, d_k, d_k, \dots, d_2, d_1$. The sum is $2(d_1 + d_2 + \dots + d_k) = 13$.
    *   If the number of digits $n$ is odd ($n=2k+1$), the digits are $d_1, d_2, \dots, d_k, d_{k+1}, d_k, \dots, d_2, d_1$. The sum is $2(d_1 + d_2 + \dots + d_k) + d_{k+1} = 13$.

### 2. Analyzing Parity of the Digit Sum

The total sum is 13, which is an **odd** number.
*   **Case: Even number of digits ($n=2k$)**
    The sum is $2 \times (\text{sum of first } k \text{ digits})$. This result must be an even number.
    Since 13 is odd, **no palindrome with an even number of digits** can have a digit sum of 13.
    *Conclusion*: We only need to consider palindromes with an **odd** number of digits.

### 3. Analyzing Odd Number of Digits

Let the number of digits be $n = 2k + 1$, where $k \ge 0$.
The digits are $d_1, d_2, \dots, d_k, d_{k+1}, d_k, \dots, d_1$.
The sum equation is:
$$2 \sum_{i=1}^k d_i + d_{k+1} = 13$$
where $d_i \in \{1, 2, \dots, 9\}$.

Let $S_k = \sum_{i=1}^k d_i$. Then $2S_k + d_{k+1} = 13$.
Since $d_{k+1} \ge 1$, we have $2S_k \le 12 \implies S_k \le 6$.
Also, since $d_i \ge 1$, $S_k \ge k$.
So, $k \le S_k \le 6$. This implies the maximum possible value for $k$ is 6.
However, we must also check the constraint on $d_{k+1}$.
$d_{k+1} = 13 - 2S_k$.
Since $d_{k+1} \le 9$, we have $13 - 2S_k \le 9 \implies 2S_k \ge 4 \implies S_k \ge 2$.
So, $S_k \in \{2, 3, 4, 5, 6\}$.

Let's iterate through possible values of $k$ (the number of pairs):

#### Case $k=0$ (1-digit number)
*   Equation: $d_1 = 13$.
*   Constraint: $d_1 \le 9$.
*   Result: **0 solutions**.

#### Case $k=1$ (3-digit number)
*   Digits: $d_1, d_2, d_1$.
*   Equation: $2d_1 + d_2 = 13$.
*   Constraints: $d_1 \in \{1, \dots, 9\}$, $d_2 \in \{1, \dots, 9\}$.
*   Possible values for $d_1$:
    *   If $d_1 = 1 \implies d_2 = 11$ (Invalid, $>9$)
    *   If $d_1 = 2 \implies d_2 = 9$ (Valid) $\rightarrow$ Number: 292
    *   If $d_1 = 3 \implies d_2 = 7$ (Valid) $\rightarrow$ Number: 373
    *   If $d_1 = 4 \implies d_2 = 5$ (Valid) $\rightarrow$ Number: 454
    *   If $d_1 = 5 \implies d_2 = 3$ (Valid) $\rightarrow$ Number: 535
    *   If $d_1 = 6 \implies d_2 = 1$ (Valid) $\rightarrow$ Number: 616
    *   If $d_1 = 7 \implies d_2 = -1$ (Invalid)
*   Count: **5 solutions**.

#### Case $k=2$ (5-digit number)
*   Digits: $d_1, d_2, d_3, d_2, d_1$.
*   Equation: $2(d_1 + d_2) + d_3 = 13$.
*   Let $S_2 = d_1 + d_2$. Then $2S_2 + d_3 = 13$.
*   Constraints: $d_1, d_2 \ge 1 \implies S_2 \ge 2$. Also $d_3 \le 9 \implies 2S_2 \ge 4 \implies S_2 \ge 2$.
*   Possible values for $S_2$:
    *   If $S_2 = 2 \implies d_3 = 9$.
        *   Pairs $(d_1, d_2)$ summing to 2: $(1, 1)$. (1 solution)
    *   If $S_2 = 3 \implies d_3 = 7$.
        *   Pairs $(d_1, d_2)$ summing to 3: $(1, 2), (2, 1)$. (2 solutions)
    *   If $S_2 = 4 \implies d_3 = 5$.
        *   Pairs $(d_1, d_2)$ summing to 4: $(1, 3), (2, 2), (3, 1)$. (3 solutions)
    *   If $S_2 = 5 \implies d_3 = 3$.
        *   Pairs $(d_1, d_2)$ summing to 5: $(1, 4), (2, 3), (3, 2), (4, 1)$. (4 solutions)
    *   If $S_2 = 6 \implies d_3 = 1$.
        *   Pairs $(d_1, d_2)$ summing to 6: $(1, 5), (2, 4), (3, 3), (4, 2), (5, 1)$. (5 solutions)
    *   If $S_2 = 7 \implies d_3 = -1$ (Invalid).
*   Total for $k=2$: $1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 = 15$.
*   Count: **15 solutions**.

#### Case $k=3$ (7-digit number)
*   Digits: $d_1, d_2, d_3, d_4, d_3, d_2, d_1$.
*   Equation: $2(d_1 + d_2 + d_3) + d_4 = 13$.
*   Let $S_3 = d_1 + d_2 + d_3$. Then $2S_3 + d_4 = 13$.
*   Constraints: $d_i \ge 1 \implies S_3 \ge 3$.
*   Possible values for $S_3$:
    *   If $S_3 = 3 \implies d_4 = 7$.
        *   Number of positive integer solutions to $d_1+d_2+d_3=3$: Using stars and bars formula $\binom{n-1}{k-1}$ where $n=3, k=3$. $\binom{2}{2} = 1$. (Solution: 1,1,1)
    *   If $S_3 = 4 \implies d_4 = 5$.
        *   Solutions to sum=4 with 3 vars: $\binom{3}{2} = 3$.
    *   If $S_3 = 5 \implies d_4 = 3$.
        *   Solutions to sum=5 with 3 vars: $\binom{4}{2} = 6$.
    *   If $S_3 = 6 \implies d_4 = 1$.
        *   Solutions to sum=6 with 3 vars: $\binom{5}{2} = 10$.
    *   If $S_3 = 7 \implies d_4 = -1$ (Invalid).
*   Total for $k=3$: $1 + 3 + 6 + 10 = 20$.
*   Count: **20 solutions**.

#### Case $k=4$ (9-digit number)
*   Digits: $d_1, \dots, d_4, d_5, \dots, d_1$.
*   Equation: $2S_4 + d_5 = 13$.
*   Constraints: $S_4 \ge 4$.
*   Possible values for $S_4$:
    *   If $S_4 = 4 \implies d_5 = 5$.
        *   Solutions to sum=4 with 4 vars: $\binom{3}{3} = 1$.
    *   If $S_4 = 5 \implies d_5 = 3$.
        *   Solutions to sum=5 with 4 vars: $\binom{4}{3} = 4$.
    *   If $S_4 = 6 \implies d_5 = 1$.
        *   Solutions to sum=6 with 4 vars: $\binom{5}{3} = 10$.
    *   If $S_4 = 7 \implies d_5 = -1$ (Invalid).
*   Total for $k=4$: $1 + 4 + 10 = 15$.
*   Count: **15 solutions**.

#### Case $k=5$ (11-digit number)
*   Digits: $d_1, \dots, d_5, d_6, \dots, d_1$.
*   Equation: $2S_5 + d_6 = 13$.
*   Constraints: $S_5 \ge 5$.
*   Possible values for $S_5$:
    *   If $S_5 = 5 \implies d_6 = 3$.
        *   Solutions to sum=5 with 5 vars: $\binom{4}{4} = 1$.
    *   If $S_5 = 6 \implies d_6 = 1$.
        *   Solutions to sum=6 with 5 vars: $\binom{5}{4} = 5$.
    *   If $S_5 = 7 \implies d_6 = -1$ (Invalid).
*   Total for $k=5$: $1 + 5 = 6$.
*   Count: **6 solutions**.

#### Case $k=6$ (13-digit number)
*   Digits: $d_1, \dots, d_6, d_7, \dots, d_1$.
*   Equation: $2S_6 + d_7 = 13$.
*   Constraints: $S_6 \ge 6$.
*   Possible values for $S_6$:
    *   If $S_6 = 6 \implies d_7 = 1$.
        *   Solutions to sum=6 with 6 vars: $\binom{5}{5} = 1$.
    *   If $S_6 = 7 \implies d_7 = -1$ (Invalid).
*   Total for $k=6$: $1$.
*   Count: **1 solution**.

#### Case $k=7$ (15-digit number)
*   Minimum sum for $k=7$ is $2(7) + 1 = 15 > 13$.
*   Count: **0 solutions**.

### 4. Summation

Now, we sum the counts from all valid cases:
*   $k=1$ (3 digits): 5
*   $k=2$ (5 digits): 15
*   $k=3$ (7 digits): 20
*   $k=4$ (9 digits): 15
*   $k=5$ (11 digits): 6
*   $k=6$ (13 digits): 1

Total = $5 + 15 + 20 + 15 + 6 + 1$
Total = $20 + 20 + 15 + 6 + 1$
Total = $40 + 15 + 6 + 1$
Total = $55 + 6 + 1$
Total = $61 + 1$
Total = $62$

### Conclusion

The number of positive integer palindromes written in base 10 with no zero digits and whose digits add up to 13 is **62**.

**Answer:** 62

*   Total for $k=4$: $1 + 4 + 10 = 15$.

#### Case $k=5$ (11-digit number)
*   Digits: $d_1, \dots, d_5, d_6, \dots, d_1$.
*   Equation: $2S_5 + d_6 = 13$.
*   Constraints: $S_5 \ge 5$.
*   Possible values for $S_5$:
    *   If $S_5 = 5 \implies d_6 = 3$.
        *   Solutions to sum=5 with 5 vars: $\binom{4}{4} = 1$.
    *   If $S_5 = 6 \implies d_6 = 1$.
        *   Solutions to sum=6 with 5 vars: $\binom{5}{4} = 5$.
    *   If $S_5 = 7 \implies d_6 = -1$ (Invalid).
*   Total for $k=5$: $1 + 5 = 6$.
*   Count: **6 solutions**.

#### Case $k=6$ (13-digit number)
*   Digits: $d_1, \dots, d_6, d_7, \dots, d_1$.
*   Equation: $2S_6 + d_7 = 13$.
*   Constraints: $S_6 \ge 6$.
*   Possible values for $S_6$:
    *   If $S_6 = 6 \implies d_7 = 1$.
        *   Solutions to sum=6 with 6 vars: $\binom{5}{5} = 1$.
    *   If $S_6 = 7 \implies d_7 = -1$ (Invalid).
*   Total for $k=6$: $1$.
*   Count: **1 solution**.

#### Case $k=7$ (15-digit number)
*   Minimum sum for $k=7$ is $2(7) + 1 = 15 > 13$.
*   Count: **0 solutions**.

### 4. Summation

Now, we sum the counts from all valid cases:
*   $k=1$ (3 digits): 5
*   $k=2$ (5 digits): 15
*   $k=3$ (7 digits): 20
*   $k=4$ (9 digits): 15
*   $k=5$ (11 digits): 6
*   $k=6$ (13 digits): 1

Total = $5 + 15 + 20 + 15 + 6 + 1$
Total = $20 + 20 + 15 + 6 + 1$
Total = $40 + 15 + 6 + 1$
Total = $55 + 6 + 1$
Total = $61 + 1$
Total = $62$

The number of positive integer palindromes written in base 10 with no zero digits and whose digits add up to 13 is **62**.

Public problem:
Find the number of positive integer palindromes written in base $10$ with no zero digits, and whose digits add up to $13$. For example, $42124$ has these properties. Recall that a palindrome is a number whose representation reads the same from left to right as from right to left.

Problem analysis:
To find the number of positive integer palindromes in base 10 with no zero digits and a digit sum of 13, we will proceed through the following logical steps:

### 1. Problem Analysis and Constraints

*   **Palindrome Property**: A number reads the same forwards and backwards. This means the sequence of digits is determined entirely by the first $\lceil n/2 \rceil$ digits, where $n$ is the total number of digits.
*   **No Zero Digits**: Every digit $d_i \in \{1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9\}$.
*   **Digit Sum**: The sum of all digits is 13.
*   **Structure**:
    *   If the number of digits $n$ is even ($n=2k$), the digits are $d_1, d_2, \dots, d_k, d_k, \dots, d_2, d_1$. The sum is $2(d_1 + d_2 + \dots + d_k) = 13$.
    *   If the number of digits $n$ is odd ($n=2k+1$), the digits are $d_1, d_2, \dots, d_k, d_{k+1}, d_k, \dots, d_2, d_1$. The sum is $2(d_1 + d_2 + \dots + d_k) + d_{k+1} = 13$.

### 2. Analyzing Parity of the Digit Sum

The total sum is 13, which is an **odd** number.
*   **Case: Even number of digits ($n=2k$)**
    The sum is $2 \times (\text{sum of first } k \text{ digits})$. This result must be an even number.
    Since 13 is odd, **no palindrome with an even number of digits** can have a digit sum of 13.
    *Conclusion*: We only need to consider palindromes with an **odd** number of digits.

### 3. Analyzing Odd Number of Digits

Let the number of digits be $n = 2k + 1$, where $k \ge 0$.
The digits are $d_1, d_2, \dots, d_k, d_{k+1}, d_k, \dots, d_1$.
The sum equation is:
$$2 \sum_{i=1}^k d_i + d_{k+1} = 13$$
where $d_i \in \{1, 2, \dots, 9\}$.

Let $S_k = \sum_{i=1}^k d_i$. Then $2S_k + d_{k+1} = 13$.
Since $d_{k+1} \ge 1$, we have $2S_k \le 12 \implies S_k \le 6$.
Also, since $d_i \ge 1$, $S_k \ge k$.
So, $k \le S_k \le 6$. This implies the maximum possible value for $k$ is 6.
However, we must also check the constraint on $d_{k+1}$.
$d_{k+1} = 13 - 2S_k$.
Since $d_{k+1} \le 9$, we have $13 - 2S_k \le 9 \implies 2S_k \ge 4 \implies S_k \ge 2$.
So, $S_k \in \{2, 3, 4, 5, 6\}$.

Let's iterate through possible values of $k$ (the number of pairs):

#### Case $k=0$ (1-digit number)
*   Equation: $d_1 = 13$.
*   Constraint: $d_1 \le 9$.
*   Result: **0 solutions**.

#### Case $k=1$ (3-digit number)
*   Digits: $d_1, d_2, d_1$.
*   Equation: $2d_1 + d_2 = 13$.
*   Constraints: $d_1 \in \{1, \dots, 9\}$, $d_2 \in \{1, \dots, 9\}$.
*   Possible values for $d_1$:
    *   If $d_1 = 1 \implies d_2 = 11$ (Invalid, $>9$)
    *   If $d_1 = 2 \implies d_2 = 9$ (Valid) $\rightarrow$ Number: 292
    *   If $d_1 = 3 \implies d_2 = 7$ (Valid) $\rightarrow$ Number: 373
    *   If $d_1 = 4 \implies d_2 = 5$ (Valid) $\rightarrow$ Number: 454
    *   If $d_1 = 5 \implies d_2 = 3$ (Valid) $\rightarrow$ Number: 535
    *   If $d_1 = 6 \implies d_2 = 1$ (Valid) $\rightarrow$ Number: 616
    *   If $d_1 = 7 \implies d_2 = -1$ (Invalid)
*   Count: **5 solutions**.

#### Case $k=2$ (5-digit number)
*   Digits: $d_1, d_2, d_3, d_2, d_1$.
*   Equation: $2(d_1 + d_2) + d_3 = 13$.
*   Let $S_2 = d_1 + d_2$. Then $2S_2 + d_3 = 13$.
*   Constraints: $d_1, d_2 \ge 1 \implies S_2 \ge 2$. Also $d_3 \le 9 \implies 2S_2 \ge 4 \implies S_2 \ge 2$.
*   Possible values for $S_2$:
    *   If $S_2 = 2 \implies d_3 = 9$.
        *   Pairs $(d_1, d_2)$ summing to 2: $(1, 1)$. (1 solution)
    *   If $S_2 = 3 \implies d_3 = 7$.
        *   Pairs $(d_1, d_2)$ summing to 3: $(1, 2), (2, 1)$. (2 solutions)
    *   If $S_2 = 4 \implies d_3 = 5$.
        *   Pairs $(d_1, d_2)$ summing to 4: $(1, 3), (2, 2), (3, 1)$. (3 solutions)
    *   If $S_2 = 5 \implies d_3 = 3$.
        *   Pairs $(d_1, d_2)$ summing to 5: $(1, 4), (2, 3), (3, 2), (4, 1)$. (4 solutions)
    *   If $S_2 = 6 \implies d_3 = 1$.
        *   Pairs $(d_1, d_2)$ summing to 6: $(1, 5), (2, 4), (3, 3), (4, 2), (5, 1)$. (5 solutions)
    *   If $S_2 = 7 \implies d_3 = -1$ (Invalid).

    *   If $S_3 = 4 \implies d_4 = 5$.

To find the number of positive integer palindromes in base 10 with no zero digits and a digit sum of 13, we analyze the structure of such numbers based on the number of digits.

### 1. Structure and Parity Analysis
Let $n$ be the number of digits in the palindrome.
- If $n$ is even ($n=2k$), the digits are symmetric: $d_1, d_2, \dots, d_k, d_k, \dots, d_2, d_1$. The sum of the digits is $2(d_1 + d_2 + \dots + d_k)$. This sum must be an even number. Since the target sum is 13 (an odd number), **no palindrome with an even number of digits** satisfies the condition.
- If $n$ is odd ($n=2k+1$), the digits are $d_1, d_2, \dots, d_k, d_{k+1}, d_k, \dots, d_2, d_1$. The sum of the digits is $2(d_1 + d_2 + \dots + d_k) + d_{k+1} = 13$.

Let $S_k = \sum_{i=1}^k d_i$. The equation becomes:
$$2S_k + d_{k+1} = 13$$
where $d_i \in \{1, 2, \dots, 9\}$ for all $i$.

From the equation, $d_{k+1} = 13 - 2S_k$.
Since $1 \le d_{k+1} \le 9$:
1. $13 - 2S_k \ge 1 \implies 2S_k \le 12 \implies S_k \le 6$.
2. $13 - 2S_k \le 9 \implies 2S_k \ge 4 \implies S_k \ge 2$.

Also, since each $d_i \ge 1$, we must have $S_k \ge k$.
Thus, for a given $k$, valid solutions exist only if $k \le S_k \le 6$. This implies $k$ can range from 1 to 6 (since for $k=0$, $S_0=0$ which is not $\ge 2$, and for $k \ge 7$, $S_k \ge 7$ which violates $S_k \le 6$).

We will calculate the number of solutions for each valid $k$ (where $n = 2k+1$). The number of ways to choose $d_1, \dots, d_k$ such that their sum is $S_k$ is given by the stars and bars formula for positive integers: $\binom{S_k - 1}{k - 1}$.

### 2. Case-by-Case Calculation

**Case $k=1$ (3-digit numbers):**
- Equation: $2S_1 + d_2 = 13$.
- Possible $S_1$: Since $1 \le S_1 \le 6$ and $S_1 \ge 1$.
- Valid $S_1$ values where $d_2 = 13 - 2S_1 \in [1, 9]$:
  - $S_1 = 2 \implies d_2 = 9$. Ways: $\binom{2-1}{1-1} = \binom{1}{0} = 1$.
  - $S_1 = 3 \implies d_2 = 7$. Ways: $\binom{3-1}{1-1} = \binom{2}{0} = 1$.
  - $S_1 = 4 \implies d_2 = 5$. Ways: $\binom{4-1}{1-1} = \binom{3}{0} = 1$.
  - $S_1 = 5 \implies d_2 = 3$. Ways: $\binom{5-1}{1-1} = \binom{4}{0} = 1$.
  - $S_1 = 6 \implies d_2 = 1$. Ways: $\binom{6-1}{1-1} = \binom{5}{0} = 1$.
- Total for $k=1$: $1+1+1+1+1 = 5$.

**Case $k=2$ (5-digit numbers):**
- Equation: $2S_2 + d_3 = 13$.
- Constraints: $2 \le S_2 \le 6$.
- Valid $S_2$ values:
  - $S_2 = 2 \implies d_3 = 9$. Ways: $\binom{2-1}{2-1} = \binom{1}{1} = 1$.
  - $S_2 = 3 \implies d_3 = 7$. Ways: $\binom{3-1}{2-1} = \binom{2}{1} = 2$.
  - $S_2 = 4 \implies d_3 = 5$. Ways: $\binom{4-1}{2-1} = \binom{3}{1} = 3$.
  - $S_2 = 5 \implies d_3 = 3$. Ways: $\binom{5-1}{2-1} = \binom{4}{1} = 4$.
  - $S_2 = 6 \implies d_3 = 1$. Ways: $\binom{6-1}{2-1} = \binom{5}{1} = 5$.
- Total for $k=2$: $1+2+3+4+5 = 15$.

**Case $k=3$ (7-digit numbers):**
- Equation: $2S_3 + d_4 = 13$.
- Constraints: $3 \le S_3 \le 6$.
- Valid $S_3$ values:
  - $S_3 = 3 \implies d_4 = 7$. Ways: $\binom{3-1}{3-1} = \binom{2}{2} = 1$.
  - $S_3 = 4 \implies d_4 = 5$. Ways: $\binom{4-1}{3-1} = \binom{3}{2} = 3$.
  - $S_3 = 5 \implies d_4 = 3$. Ways: $\binom{5-1}{3-1} = \binom{4}{2} = 6$.
  - $S_3 = 6 \implies d_4 = 1$. Ways: $\binom{6-1}{3-1} = \binom{5}{2} = 10$.
- Total for $k=3$: $1+3+6+10 = 20$.

**Case $k=4$ (9-digit numbers):**
- Equation: $2S_4 + d_5 = 13$.
- Constraints: $4 \le S_4 \le 6$.
- Valid $S_4$ values:
  - $S_4 = 4 \implies d_5 = 5$. Ways: $\binom{4-1}{4-1} = \binom{3}{3} = 1$.
  - $S_4 = 5 \implies d_5 = 3$. Ways: $\binom{5-1}{4-1} = \binom{4}{3} = 4$.
  - $S_4 = 6 \implies d_5 = 1$. Ways: $\binom{6-1}{4-1} = \binom{5}{3} = 10$.
- Total for $k=4$: $1+4+10 = 15$.

**Case $k=5$ (11-digit numbers):**
- Equation: $2S_5 + d_6 = 13$.
- Constraints: $5 \le S_5 \le 6$.
- Valid $S_5$ values:
  - $S_5 = 5 \implies d_6 = 3$. Ways: $\binom{5-1}{5-1} = \binom{4}{4} = 1$.
  - $S_5 = 6 \implies d_6 = 1$. Ways: $\binom{6-1}{5-1} = \binom{5}{4} = 5$.
- Total for $k=5$: $1+5 = 6$.

**Case $k=6$ (13-digit numbers):**
- Equation: $2S_6 + d_7 = 13$.
- Constraints: $6 \le S_6 \le 6$.
- Valid $S_6$ values:
  - $S_6 = 6 \implies d_7 = 1$. Ways: $\binom{6-1}{6-1} = \binom{5}{5} = 1$.
- Total for $k=6$: $1$.

Public problem:
Find the number of positive integer palindromes written in base $10$ with no zero digits, and whose digits add up to $13$. For example, $42124$ has these properties. Recall that a palindrome is a number whose representation reads the same from left to right as from right to left.

Candidate to verify:
To find the number of positive integer palindromes in base 10 with no zero digits and a digit sum of 13, we analyze the structure of such numbers based on the number of digits.

### 1. Structure and Parity Analysis
Let $n$ be the number of digits in the palindrome.
- If $n$ is even ($n=2k$), the digits are symmetric: $d_1, d_2, \dots, d_k, d_k, \dots, d_2, d_1$. The sum of the digits is $2(d_1 + d_2 + \dots + d_k)$. This sum must be an even number. Since the target sum is 13 (an odd number), **no palindrome with an even number of digits** satisfies the condition.
- If $n$ is odd ($n=2k+1$), the digits are $d_1, d_2, \dots, d_k, d_{k+1}, d_k, \dots, d_2, d_1$. The sum of the digits is $2(d_1 + d_2 + \dots + d_k) + d_{k+1} = 13$.

Let $S_k = \sum_{i=1}^k d_i$. The equation becomes:
$$2S_k + d_{k+1} = 13$$
where $d_i \in \{1, 2, \dots, 9\}$ for all $i$.

From the equation, $d_{k+1} = 13 - 2S_k$.
Since $1 \le d_{k+1} \le 9$:
1. $13 - 2S_k \ge 1 \implies 2S_k \le 12 \implies S_k \le 6$.
2. $13 - 2S_k \le 9 \implies 2S_k \ge 4 \implies S_k \ge 2$.

Also, since each $d_i \ge 1$, we must have $S_k \ge k$.
Thus, for a given $k$, valid solutions exist only if $k \le S_k \le 6$. This implies $k$ can range from 1 to 6 (since for $k=0$, $S_0=0$ which is not $\ge 2$, and for $k \ge 7$, $S_k \ge 7$ which violates $S_k \le 6$).

We will calculate the number of solutions for each valid $k$ (where $n = 2k+1$). The number of ways to choose $d_1, \dots, d_k$ such that their sum is $S_k$ is given by the stars and bars formula for positive integers: $\binom{S_k - 1}{k - 1}$.

### 2. Case-by-Case Calculation

**Case $k=1$ (3-digit numbers):**
- Equation: $2S_1 + d_2 = 13$.
- Possible $S_1$: Since $1 \le S_1 \le 6$ and $S_1 \ge 1$.
- Valid $S_1$ values where $d_2 = 13 - 2S_1 \in [1, 9]$:
  - $S_1 = 2 \implies d_2 = 9$. Ways: $\binom{2-1}{1-1} = \binom{1}{0} = 1$.
  - $S_1 = 3 \implies d_2 = 7$. Ways: $\binom{3-1}{1-1} = \binom{2}{0} = 1$.
  - $S_1 = 4 \implies d_2 = 5$. Ways: $\binom{4-1}{1-1} = \binom{3}{0} = 1$.
  - $S_1 = 5 \implies d_2 = 3$. Ways: $\binom{5-1}{1-1} = \binom{4}{0} = 1$.
  - $S_1 = 6 \implies d_2 = 1$. Ways: $\binom{6-1}{1-1} = \binom{5}{0} = 1$.
- Total for $k=1$: $1+1+1+1+1 = 5$.

**Case $k=2$ (5-digit numbers):**
- Equation: $2S_2 + d_3 = 13$.
- Constraints: $2 \le S_2 \le 6$.
- Valid $S_2$ values:
  - $S_2 = 2 \implies d_3 = 9$. Ways: $\binom{2-1}{2-1} = \binom{1}{1} = 1$.
  - $S_2 = 3 \implies d_3 = 7$. Ways: $\binom{3-1}{2-1} = \binom{2}{1} = 2$.
  - $S_2 = 4 \implies d_3 = 5$. Ways: $\binom{4-1}{2-1} = \binom{3}{1} = 3$.
  - $S_2 = 5 \implies d_3 = 3$. Ways: $\binom{5-1}{2-1} = \binom{4}{1} = 4$.
  - $S_2 = 6 \implies d_3 = 1$. Ways: $\binom{6-1}{2-1} = \binom{5}{1} = 5$.
- Total for $k=2$: $1+2+3+4+5 = 15$.

**Case $k=3$ (7-digit numbers):**
- Equation: $2S_3 + d_4 = 13$.
- Constraints: $3 \le S_3 \le 6$.
- Valid $S_3$ values:

  - $S_3 = 4 \implies d_4 = 5$. Ways: $\binom{4-1}{3-1} = \binom{3}{2} = 3$.
  - $S_3 = 5 \implies d_4 = 3$. Ways: $\binom{5-1}{3-1} = \binom{4}{2} = 6$.
  - $S_3 = 6 \implies d_4 = 1$. Ways: $\binom{6-1}{3-1} = \binom{5}{2} = 10$.
- Total for $k=3$: $1+3+6+10 = 20$.

To find the number of positive integer palindromes in base 10 with no zero digits and a digit sum of 13, we proceed with the following logical steps:

### 1. Analysis of Palindrome Structure and Parity
Let $n$ be the number of digits in the palindrome.
- **Even number of digits ($n=2k$):** The digits are symmetric ($d_1, \dots, d_k, d_k, \dots, d_1$). The sum of the digits is $2 \sum_{i=1}^k d_i$. This sum must be even. Since the target sum is 13 (an odd number), no palindrome with an even number of digits can satisfy the condition.
- **Odd number of digits ($n=2k+1$):** The digits are symmetric around a central digit ($d_1, \dots, d_k, d_{k+1}, d_k, \dots, d_1$). The sum of the digits is $2 \sum_{i=1}^k d_i + d_{k+1} = 13$.

Let $S_k = \sum_{i=1}^k d_i$. The equation becomes:
$$2S_k + d_{k+1} = 13$$
where each digit $d_i \in \{1, 2, \dots, 9\}$.

### 2. Constraints on $S_k$ and $d_{k+1}$
From the equation, $d_{k+1} = 13 - 2S_k$.
Since $1 \le d_{k+1} \le 9$:
1.  $13 - 2S_k \ge 1 \implies 2S_k \le 12 \implies S_k \le 6$.
2.  $13 - 2S_k \le 9 \implies 2S_k \ge 4 \implies S_k \ge 2$.

Additionally, since each $d_i \ge 1$, the sum of $k$ digits must satisfy $S_k \ge k$.
Combining these, for a given $k$, valid solutions exist if $k \le S_k \le 6$.
This implies $k$ can range from 1 to 6.
- If $k=0$ (1-digit number), $S_0=0$, which is not $\ge 2$.
- If $k \ge 7$, $S_k \ge 7$, which violates $S_k \le 6$.

The number of ways to choose $k$ positive integers $d_1, \dots, d_k$ that sum to $S_k$ is given by the stars and bars formula: $\binom{S_k - 1}{k - 1}$.

### 3. Case-by-Case Calculation

**Case $k=1$ (3-digit numbers):**
- Range for $S_1$: $1 \le S_1 \le 6$.
- Valid $S_1$ values (where $d_2 = 13-2S_1 \in [1,9]$): $2, 3, 4, 5, 6$.
- Ways: $\sum_{S_1=2}^{6} \binom{S_1-1}{0} = 1+1+1+1+1 = 5$.

**Case $k=2$ (5-digit numbers):**
- Range for $S_2$: $2 \le S_2 \le 6$.
- Valid $S_2$ values: $2, 3, 4, 5, 6$.
- Ways: $\sum_{S_2=2}^{6} \binom{S_2-1}{1} = \binom{1}{1} + \binom{2}{1} + \binom{3}{1} + \binom{4}{1} + \binom{5}{1} = 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 = 15$.

**Case $k=3$ (7-digit numbers):**
- Range for $S_3$: $3 \le S_3 \le 6$.
- Valid $S_3$ values: $3, 4, 5, 6$.
- Ways: $\sum_{S_3=3}^{6} \binom{S_3-1}{2} = \binom{2}{2} + \binom{3}{2} + \binom{4}{2} + \binom{5}{2} = 1 + 3 + 6 + 10 = 20$.

**Case $k=4$ (9-digit numbers):**
- Range for $S_4$: $4 \le S_4 \le 6$.
- Valid $S_4$ values: $4, 5, 6$.
- Ways: $\sum_{S_4=4}^{6} \binom{S_4-1}{3} = \binom{3}{3} + \binom{4}{3} + \binom{5}{3} = 1 + 4 + 10 = 15$.

**Case $k=5$ (11-digit numbers):**
- Range for $S_5$: $5 \le S_5 \le 6$.
- Valid $S_5$ values: $5, 6$.
- Ways: $\sum_{S_5=5}^{6} \binom{S_5-1}{4} = \binom{4}{4} + \binom{5}{4} = 1 + 5 = 6$.

**Case $k=6$ (13-digit numbers):**
- Range for $S_6$: $6 \le S_6 \le 6$.
- Valid $S_6$ values: $6$.
- Ways: $\binom{6-1}{5} = \binom{5}{5} = 1$.

### 4. Final Summation
Total number of palindromes = $5 + 15 + 20 + 15 + 6 + 1 = 62$.

user
Public problem:
A hemisphere with radius $200$ sits on top of a horizontal circular disk with radius $200,$ and the hemisphere and disk have the same center. Let $\mathcal T$ be the region of points P in the disk such that a sphere of radius $42$ can be placed on top of the disk at $P$ and lie completely inside the hemisphere. The area of $\mathcal T$ divided by the area of the disk is $\tfrac pq,$ where $p$ and $q$ are relatively prime positive integers. Find $p+q.$
assistantfinish_reason=stop
### 1. Problem Analysis and Geometric Setup

Let's define the coordinate system and the geometric objects involved:
*   **The Disk ($D$):** A horizontal circular disk of radius $R = 200$ lying in the $xy$-plane ($z=0$). Its center is at the origin $(0,0,0)$.
*   **The Hemisphere ($H$):** A hemisphere of radius $R = 200$ sitting on top of the disk. Its equation is $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 \le 200^2$ with $z \ge 0$.
*   **The Small Sphere ($S$):** A sphere of radius $r = 42$.
*   **The Point $P$:** A point in the disk $D$ where the small sphere is placed. Let $P = (x, y, 0)$. Since the sphere is placed "on top of the disk at $P$", the bottom of the sphere touches the disk at $P$. Thus, the center of the small sphere, let's call it $C$, is located at $(x, y, r) = (x, y, 42)$.

**The Condition:**
The small sphere must lie **completely inside** the hemisphere.
This means every point on the small sphere must satisfy the inequality of the hemisphere: $X^2 + Y^2 + Z^2 \le R^2$.

### 2. Deriving the Constraint on Point $P$

Let the center of the small sphere be $C = (x, y, r)$.
The distance from the origin $O(0,0,0)$ to the center $C$ is $d = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + r^2}$.
For the sphere of radius $r$ centered at $C$ to be entirely contained within the sphere of radius $R$ centered at $O$, the distance from the origin to the farthest point on the small sphere must be less than or equal to $R$.
The farthest point on the small sphere from the origin lies on the line connecting the origin to $C$, at a distance $d + r$ from the origin.

Therefore, the condition is:
$$d + r \le R$$
Substituting $d = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + r^2}$:
$$\sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + r^2} + r \le R$$

Let $\rho = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ be the distance of point $P$ from the center of the disk. The inequality becomes:
$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} + r \le R$$

Now, we solve for $\rho$:
$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} \le R - r$$
Since $R = 200$ and $r = 42$, $R - r = 158 > 0$, so we can square both sides without changing the inequality direction:
$$\rho^2 + r^2 \le (R - r)^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le (R - r)^2 - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le R^2 - 2Rr + r^2 - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le R^2 - 2Rr$$
$$\rho \le \sqrt{R^2 - 2Rr}$$

### 3. Calculating the Area of Region $\mathcal{T}$

The region $\mathcal{T}$ consists of all points $P$ in the disk such that their distance from the center $\rho$ satisfies the condition derived above.
Since the condition depends only on the distance from the center, $\mathcal{T}$ is a circular disk centered at the origin with radius $\rho_{max} = \sqrt{R^2 - 2Rr}$.

Let's calculate the value of $\rho_{max}^2$:
Given $R = 200$ and $r = 42$:
$$R^2 = 200^2 = 40000$$
$$2Rr = 2 \times 200 \times 42 = 400 \times 42 = 16800$$
$$\rho_{max}^2 = 40000 - 16800 = 23200$$

The area of region $\mathcal{T}$ is:
$$\text{Area}(\mathcal{T}) = \pi (\rho_{max})^2 = \pi (23200)$$

The area of the original disk $D$ is:
$$\text{Area}(D) = \pi R^2 = \pi (200^2) = \pi (40000)$$

### 4. Calculating the Ratio

We need the ratio of the area of $\mathcal{T}$ to the area of the disk:
$$\frac{\text{Area}(\mathcal{T})}{\text{Area}(D)} = \frac{23200 \pi}{40000 \pi} = \frac{23200}{40000}$$

Simplify the fraction:
$$\frac{23200}{40000} = \frac{232}{400}$$
Divide numerator and denominator by 8:
$$232 \div 8 = 29$$
$$400 \div 8 = 50$$
So, the ratio is:
$$\frac{29}{50}$$

### 5. Final Verification and Conclusion

We have the fraction $\frac{p}{q} = \frac{29}{50}$.
*   $p = 29$
*   $q = 50$

Check if $p$ and $q$ are relatively prime:
*   $29$ is a prime number.
*   $50 = 2 \times 5^2$.
*   They share no common factors other than 1. Thus, $\gcd(29, 50) = 1$.

The problem asks for $p + q$:
$$p + q = 29 + 50 = 79$$

**Answer:** 79

Public problem:
A hemisphere with radius $200$ sits on top of a horizontal circular disk with radius $200,$ and the hemisphere and disk have the same center. Let $\mathcal T$ be the region of points P in the disk such that a sphere of radius $42$ can be placed on top of the disk at $P$ and lie completely inside the hemisphere. The area of $\mathcal T$ divided by the area of the disk is $\tfrac pq,$ where $p$ and $q$ are relatively prime positive integers. Find $p+q.$

Problem analysis:
### 1. Problem Analysis and Geometric Setup

Let's define the coordinate system and the geometric objects involved:
*   **The Disk ($D$):** A horizontal circular disk of radius $R = 200$ lying in the $xy$-plane ($z=0$). Its center is at the origin $(0,0,0)$.
*   **The Hemisphere ($H$):** A hemisphere of radius $R = 200$ sitting on top of the disk. Its equation is $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 \le 200^2$ with $z \ge 0$.
*   **The Small Sphere ($S$):** A sphere of radius $r = 42$.
*   **The Point $P$:** A point in the disk $D$ where the small sphere is placed. Let $P = (x, y, 0)$. Since the sphere is placed "on top of the disk at $P$", the bottom of the sphere touches the disk at $P$. Thus, the center of the small sphere, let's call it $C$, is located at $(x, y, r) = (x, y, 42)$.

**The Condition:**
The small sphere must lie **completely inside** the hemisphere.
This means every point on the small sphere must satisfy the inequality of the hemisphere: $X^2 + Y^2 + Z^2 \le R^2$.

### 2. Deriving the Constraint on Point $P$

Let the center of the small sphere be $C = (x, y, r)$.
The distance from the origin $O(0,0,0)$ to the center $C$ is $d = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + r^2}$.
For the sphere of radius $r$ centered at $C$ to be entirely contained within the sphere of radius $R$ centered at $O$, the distance from the origin to the farthest point on the small sphere must be less than or equal to $R$.
The farthest point on the small sphere from the origin lies on the line connecting the origin to $C$, at a distance $d + r$ from the origin.

Therefore, the condition is:
$$d + r \le R$$
Substituting $d = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + r^2}$:
$$\sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + r^2} + r \le R$$

Let $\rho = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ be the distance of point $P$ from the center of the disk. The inequality becomes:
$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} + r \le R$$

Now, we solve for $\rho$:
$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} \le R - r$$
Since $R = 200$ and $r = 42$, $R - r = 158 > 0$, so we can square both sides without changing the inequality direction:
$$\rho^2 + r^2 \le (R - r)^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le (R - r)^2 - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le R^2 - 2Rr + r^2 - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le R^2 - 2Rr$$
$$\rho \le \sqrt{R^2 - 2Rr}$$

### 3. Calculating the Area of Region $\mathcal{T}$

The region $\mathcal{T}$ consists of all points $P$ in the disk such that their distance from the center $\rho$ satisfies the condition derived above.
Since the condition depends only on the distance from the center, $\mathcal{T}$ is a circular disk centered at the origin with radius $\rho_{max} = \sqrt{R^2 - 2Rr}$.

Let's calculate the value of $\rho_{max}^2$:
Given $R = 200$ and $r = 42$:
$$R^2 = 200^2 = 40000$$
$$2Rr = 2 \times 200 \times 42 = 400 \times 42 = 16800$$
$$\rho_{max}^2 = 40000 - 16800 = 23200$$

The area of region $\mathcal{T}$ is:
$$\text{Area}(\mathcal{T}) = \pi (\rho_{max})^2 = \pi (23200)$$

The area of the original disk $D$ is:
$$\text{Area}(D) = \pi R^2 = \pi (200^2) = \pi (40000)$$

### 4. Calculating the Ratio

We need the ratio of the area of $\mathcal{T}$ to the area of the disk:
$$\frac{\text{Area}(\mathcal{T})}{\text{Area}(D)} = \frac{23200 \pi}{40000 \pi} = \frac{23200}{40000}$$

Simplify the fraction:
$$\frac{23200}{40000} = \frac{232}{400}$$
Divide numerator and denominator by 8:
$$232 \div 8 = 29$$
$$400 \div 8 = 50$$
So, the ratio is:
$$\frac{29}{50}$$

### 5. Final Verification and Conclusion

We have the fraction $\frac{p}{q} = \frac{29}{50}$.
*   $p = 29$
*   $q = 50$

Check if $p$ and $q$ are relatively prime:
*   $29$ is a prime number.
*   $50 = 2 \times 5^2$.
*   They share no common factors other than 1. Thus, $\gcd(29, 50) = 1$.

The problem asks for $p + q$:
$$p + q = 29 + 50 = 79$$

**Answer:** 79
assistantfinish_reason=stop
To find the solution, we will model the geometric setup, derive the condition for the small sphere to fit inside the hemisphere, calculate the area of the valid region, and determine the required ratio.

### 1. Geometric Setup and Coordinate System

Let the center of the horizontal circular disk be the origin $(0,0,0)$ in a 3D Cartesian coordinate system.
*   **The Disk ($D$):** Lies in the $xy$-plane ($z=0$) with radius $R = 200$. The region is defined by $x^2 + y^2 \le R^2$.
*   **The Hemisphere ($H$):** Sits on top of the disk, sharing the same center. It is the upper half of a sphere of radius $R = 200$. The region is defined by $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 \le R^2$ with $z \ge 0$.
*   **The Small Sphere ($S$):** Has radius $r = 42$. It is placed on the disk at a point $P(x,y,0)$. Since it rests on the disk, its center $C$ is located directly above $P$ at a height equal to its radius. Thus, the coordinates of the center $C$ are $(x, y, r) = (x, y, 42)$.

### 2. Condition for Containment

For the small sphere $S$ to lie **completely inside** the hemisphere $H$, every point on the surface of $S$ must satisfy the inequality $X^2 + Y^2 + Z^2 \le R^2$.

The point on the small sphere farthest from the origin $O(0,0,0)$ lies on the line segment connecting the origin to the center $C$, extended by the radius $r$.
Let $\rho$ be the distance of point $P$ from the origin in the $xy$-plane, so $\rho = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$.
The distance from the origin to the center $C$ is:
$$d = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + r^2} = \sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2}$$

The distance from the origin to the farthest point on the small sphere is $d + r$.
The condition for the sphere to be inside the hemisphere is:
$$d + r \le R$$
Substituting the expression for $d$:
$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} + r \le R$$

### 3. Solving for the Valid Region $\mathcal{T}$

We solve the inequality for $\rho$:
$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} \le R - r$$

Since $R = 200$ and $r = 42$, $R - r = 158$, which is positive. We can square both sides:
$$\rho^2 + r^2 \le (R - r)^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le (R - r)^2 - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le (R^2 - 2Rr + r^2) - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le R^2 - 2Rr$$

The region $\mathcal{T}$ consists of all points $P$ in the disk satisfying this condition. Since the condition depends only on the distance $\rho$ from the center, $\mathcal{T}$ is a circular disk centered at the origin with radius $\rho_{max} = \sqrt{R^2 - 2Rr}$.

### 4. Calculating the Areas and Ratio

Given $R = 200$ and $r = 42$:
1.  Calculate $R^2$:
    $$R^2 = 200^2 = 40,000$$
2.  Calculate $2Rr$:
    $$2Rr = 2 \times 200 \times 42 = 400 \times 42 = 16,800$$
3.  Calculate $\rho_{max}^2$ (which is proportional to the area of $\mathcal{T}$):
    $$\rho_{max}^2 = 40,000 - 16,800 = 23,200$$

The area of region $\mathcal{T}$ is:
$$\text{Area}(\mathcal{T}) = \pi \rho_{max}^2 = 23,200\pi$$

The area of the original disk $D$ is:
$$\text{Area}(D) = \pi R^2 = 40,000\pi$$

The ratio of the areas is:
$$\frac{\text{Area}(\mathcal{T})}{\text{Area}(D)} = \frac{23,200\pi}{40,000\pi} = \frac{23,200}{40,000}$$

Simplify the fraction:
$$\frac{23,200}{40,000} = \frac{232}{400}$$
Divide numerator and denominator by their greatest common divisor. Both are divisible by 8:
$$232 \div 8 = 29$$
$$400 \div 8 = 50$$
So, the ratio is:
$$\frac{29}{50}$$

### 5. Final Calculation

We have $\frac{p}{q} = \frac{29}{50}$.
*   $p = 29$
*   $q = 50$

Check if $p$ and $q$ are relatively prime:
$29$ is a prime number. $50 = 2 \times 5^2$. They share no common factors. Thus, $\gcd(29, 50) = 1$.

The problem asks for $p + q$:
$$p + q = 29 + 50 = 79$$
answer<answer>
\boxed{79}
final_text for this step
To find the solution, we will model the geometric setup, derive the condition for the small sphere to fit inside the hemisphere, calculate the area of the valid region, and determine the required ratio.

### 1. Geometric Setup and Coordinate System

Let the center of the horizontal circular disk be the origin $(0,0,0)$ in a 3D Cartesian coordinate system.
*   **The Disk ($D$):** Lies in the $xy$-plane ($z=0$) with radius $R = 200$. The region is defined by $x^2 + y^2 \le R^2$.

*   **The Small Sphere ($S$):** Has radius $r = 42$. It is placed on the disk at a point $P(x,y,0)$. Since it rests on the disk, its center $C$ is located directly above $P$ at a height equal to its radius. Thus, the coordinates of the center $C$ are $(x, y, r) = (x, y, 42)$.

### 2. Condition for Containment

The distance from the origin to the farthest point on the small sphere is $d + r$.

$$d + r \le R$$

$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} + r \le R$$

### 3. Solving for the Valid Region $\mathcal{T}$

We solve the inequality for $\rho$:
$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} \le R - r$$

Since $R = 200$ and $r = 42$, $R - r = 158$, which is positive. We can square both sides:
$$\rho^2 + r^2 \le (R - r)^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le (R - r)^2 - r^2$$

$$\rho^2 \le R^2 - 2Rr$$

    $$R^2 = 200^2 = 40,000$$

    $$\rho_{max}^2 = 40,000 - 16,800 = 23,200$$

The area of region $\mathcal{T}$ is:
$$\text{Area}(\mathcal{T}) = \pi \rho_{max}^2 = 23,200\pi$$

The area of the original disk $D$ is:
$$\text{Area}(D) = \pi R^2 = 40,000\pi$$

The ratio of the areas is:
$$\frac{\text{Area}(\mathcal{T})}{\text{Area}(D)} = \frac{23,200\pi}{40,000\pi} = \frac{23,200}{40,000}$$

Simplify the fraction:
$$\frac{23,200}{40,000} = \frac{232}{400}$$
Divide numerator and denominator by their greatest common divisor. Both are divisible by 8:
$$232 \div 8 = 29$$
$$400 \div 8 = 50$$
So, the ratio is:
$$\frac{29}{50}$$

### 5. Final Calculation

We have $\frac{p}{q} = \frac{29}{50}$.
*   $p = 29$
*   $q = 50$

Check if $p$ and $q$ are relatively prime:
$29$ is a prime number. $50 = 2 \times 5^2$. They share no common factors. Thus, $\gcd(29, 50) = 1$.

The problem asks for $p + q$:
$$p + q = 29 + 50 = 79$$

Let the center of the horizontal circular disk be the origin $(0,0,0)$ in a 3D Cartesian coordinate system.

For the small sphere $S$ to lie **completely inside** the hemisphere $H$, every point on the surface of $S$ must satisfy the inequality $X^2 + Y^2 + Z^2 \le R^2$.

The point on the small sphere farthest from the origin $O(0,0,0)$ lies on the line segment connecting the origin to the center $C$, extended by the radius $r$.
Let $\rho$ be the distance of point $P$ from the origin in the $xy$-plane, so $\rho = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$.
The distance from the origin to the center $C$ is:
$$d = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + r^2} = \sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2}$$

The distance from the origin to the farthest point on the small sphere is $d + r$.
The condition for the sphere to be inside the hemisphere is:
$$d + r \le R$$
Substituting the expression for $d$:
$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} + r \le R$$

### 3. Solving for the Valid Region $\mathcal{T}$

We solve the inequality for $\rho$:
$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} \le R - r$$

Since $R = 200$ and $r = 42$, $R - r = 158$, which is positive. We can square both sides:
$$\rho^2 + r^2 \le (R - r)^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le (R - r)^2 - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le (R^2 - 2Rr + r^2) - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le R^2 - 2Rr$$

The region $\mathcal{T}$ consists of all points $P$ in the disk satisfying this condition. Since the condition depends only on the distance $\rho$ from the center, $\mathcal{T}$ is a circular disk centered at the origin with radius $\rho_{max} = \sqrt{R^2 - 2Rr}$.

### 4. Calculating the Areas and Ratio

Given $R = 200$ and $r = 42$:
1.  Calculate $R^2$:
    $$R^2 = 200^2 = 40,000$$
2.  Calculate $2Rr$:
    $$2Rr = 2 \times 200 \times 42 = 400 \times 42 = 16,800$$
3.  Calculate $\rho_{max}^2$ (which is proportional to the area of $\mathcal{T}$):
    $$\rho_{max}^2 = 40,000 - 16,800 = 23,200$$

The area of region $\mathcal{T}$ is:
$$\text{Area}(\mathcal{T}) = \pi \rho_{max}^2 = 23,200\pi$$

The area of the original disk $D$ is:
$$\text{Area}(D) = \pi R^2 = 40,000\pi$$

The ratio of the areas is:
$$\frac{\text{Area}(\mathcal{T})}{\text{Area}(D)} = \frac{23,200\pi}{40,000\pi} = \frac{23,200}{40,000}$$

Simplify the fraction:
$$\frac{23,200}{40,000} = \frac{232}{400}$$
Divide numerator and denominator by their greatest common divisor. Both are divisible by 8:
$$232 \div 8 = 29$$
$$400 \div 8 = 50$$
So, the ratio is:
$$\frac{29}{50}$$

### 5. Final Calculation

We have $\frac{p}{q} = \frac{29}{50}$.
*   $p = 29$
*   $q = 50$

Check if $p$ and $q$ are relatively prime:
$29$ is a prime number. $50 = 2 \times 5^2$. They share no common factors. Thus, $\gcd(29, 50) = 1$.

The problem asks for $p + q$:
$$p + q = 29 + 50 = 79$$
answer<answer>
\boxed{79}

Public problem:
A hemisphere with radius $200$ sits on top of a horizontal circular disk with radius $200,$ and the hemisphere and disk have the same center. Let $\mathcal T$ be the region of points P in the disk such that a sphere of radius $42$ can be placed on top of the disk at $P$ and lie completely inside the hemisphere. The area of $\mathcal T$ divided by the area of the disk is $\tfrac pq,$ where $p$ and $q$ are relatively prime positive integers. Find $p+q.$

Current candidate:
To find the solution, we will model the geometric setup, derive the condition for the small sphere to fit inside the hemisphere, calculate the area of the valid region, and determine the required ratio.

### 1. Geometric Setup and Coordinate System

Let the center of the horizontal circular disk be the origin $(0,0,0)$ in a 3D Cartesian coordinate system.
*   **The Disk ($D$):** Lies in the $xy$-plane ($z=0$) with radius $R = 200$. The region is defined by $x^2 + y^2 \le R^2$.
*   **The Hemisphere ($H$):** Sits on top of the disk, sharing the same center. It is the upper half of a sphere of radius $R = 200$. The region is defined by $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 \le R^2$ with $z \ge 0$.
*   **The Small Sphere ($S$):** Has radius $r = 42$. It is placed on the disk at a point $P(x,y,0)$. Since it rests on the disk, its center $C$ is located directly above $P$ at a height equal to its radius. Thus, the coordinates of the center $C$ are $(x, y, r) = (x, y, 42)$.

### 2. Condition for Containment

For the small sphere $S$ to lie **completely inside** the hemisphere $H$, every point on the surface of $S$ must satisfy the inequality $X^2 + Y^2 + Z^2 \le R^2$.

The point on the small sphere farthest from the origin $O(0,0,0)$ lies on the line segment connecting the origin to the center $C$, extended by the radius $r$.
Let $\rho$ be the distance of point $P$ from the origin in the $xy$-plane, so $\rho = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$.
The distance from the origin to the center $C$ is:
$$d = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + r^2} = \sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2}$$

The distance from the origin to the farthest point on the small sphere is $d + r$.
The condition for the sphere to be inside the hemisphere is:
$$d + r \le R$$
Substituting the expression for $d$:
$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} + r \le R$$

### 3. Solving for the Valid Region $\mathcal{T}$

We solve the inequality for $\rho$:
$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} \le R - r$$

Since $R = 200$ and $r = 42$, $R - r = 158$, which is positive. We can square both sides:
$$\rho^2 + r^2 \le (R - r)^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le (R - r)^2 - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le (R^2 - 2Rr + r^2) - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le R^2 - 2Rr$$

The region $\mathcal{T}$ consists of all points $P$ in the disk satisfying this condition. Since the condition depends only on the distance $\rho$ from the center, $\mathcal{T}$ is a circular disk centered at the origin with radius $\rho_{max} = \sqrt{R^2 - 2Rr}$.

### 4. Calculating the Areas and Ratio

Given $R = 200$ and $r = 42$:
1.  Calculate $R^2$:
    $$R^2 = 200^2 = 40,000$$
2.  Calculate $2Rr$:
    $$2Rr = 2 \times 200 \times 42 = 400 \times 42 = 16,800$$
3.  Calculate $\rho_{max}^2$ (which is proportional to the area of $\mathcal{T}$):
    $$\rho_{max}^2 = 40,000 - 16,800 = 23,200$$

The area of region $\mathcal{T}$ is:
$$\text{Area}(\mathcal{T}) = \pi \rho_{max}^2 = 23,200\pi$$

The area of the original disk $D$ is:
$$\text{Area}(D) = \pi R^2 = 40,000\pi$$

The ratio of the areas is:
$$\frac{\text{Area}(\mathcal{T})}{\text{Area}(D)} = \frac{23,200\pi}{40,000\pi} = \frac{23,200}{40,000}$$

Simplify the fraction:
$$\frac{23,200}{40,000} = \frac{232}{400}$$
Divide numerator and denominator by their greatest common divisor. Both are divisible by 8:
$$232 \div 8 = 29$$
$$400 \div 8 = 50$$
So, the ratio is:
$$\frac{29}{50}$$

### 5. Final Calculation

We have $\frac{p}{q} = \frac{29}{50}$.
*   $p = 29$
*   $q = 50$

Check if $p$ and $q$ are relatively prime:
$29$ is a prime number. $50 = 2 \times 5^2$. They share no common factors. Thus, $\gcd(29, 50) = 1$.

The problem asks for $p + q$:
$$p + q = 29 + 50 = 79$$

\boxed{79}

assistantfinish_reason=stop
The problem asks for the area of the region $\mathcal{T}$ on a disk of radius $R=200$ where a sphere of radius $r=42$ can be placed such that it sits on the disk and lies completely inside a hemisphere of radius $R=200$ centered at the same point.

**1. Geometric Setup:**
Let the center of the disk and the hemisphere be the origin $(0,0,0)$.
The hemisphere is defined by $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 \le R^2$ with $z \ge 0$.
The disk lies in the plane $z=0$ and is defined by $x^2 + y^2 \le R^2$.
Let $P = (x, y, 0)$ be a point on the disk where the small sphere is placed.
Since the small sphere of radius $r$ sits on the disk at $P$, its center $C$ must be at $(x, y, r)$.

**2. Condition for Containment:**
For the small sphere to lie completely inside the hemisphere, every point on the small sphere must satisfy the hemisphere's inequality.
The farthest point on the small sphere from the origin lies on the line segment connecting the origin to the center $C$, extended by the radius $r$.
The distance from the origin to the center $C$ is $d = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + r^2}$.
The distance from the origin to the farthest point on the small sphere is $d + r$.
The condition for the sphere to be inside the hemisphere is:
$$d + r \le R$$
Substituting $d$:
$$\sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + r^2} + r \le R$$

**3. Solving for the Region $\mathcal{T}$:**
Let $\rho = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ be the distance of point $P$ from the center of the disk.
The inequality becomes:
$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} \le R - r$$
Since $R = 200$ and $r = 42$, $R - r = 158 > 0$, so we can square both sides:
$$\rho^2 + r^2 \le (R - r)^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le (R - r)^2 - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le R^2 - 2Rr + r^2 - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le R^2 - 2Rr$$
$$\rho^2 \le R(R - 2r)$$

**4. Calculation:**
Given $R = 200$ and $r = 42$:
$$R - 2r = 200 - 2(42) = 200 - 84 = 116$$
$$\rho_{max}^2 = 200 \times 116 = 23200$$
The region $\mathcal{T}$ is a disk of radius $\rho_{max} = \sqrt{23200}$.
The area of $\mathcal{T}$ is:
$$\text{Area}(\mathcal{T}) = \pi \rho_{max}^2 = 23200\pi$$
The area of the original disk is:
$$\text{Area}(\text{Disk}) = \pi R^2 = \pi (200)^2 = 40000\pi$$

**5. Ratio and Final Answer:**
The ratio of the areas is:
$$\frac{\text{Area}(\mathcal{T})}{\text{Area}(\text{Disk})} = \frac{23200\pi}{40000\pi} = \frac{23200}{40000} = \frac{232}{400}$$
Simplify the fraction by dividing numerator and denominator by their greatest common divisor.
Both are divisible by 8:
$$232 \div 8 = 29$$
$$400 \div 8 = 50$$
So the ratio is $\frac{29}{50}$.
Here $p = 29$ and $q = 50$.
Check if they are relatively prime: $29$ is a prime number and does not divide $50$. Thus, $\gcd(29, 50) = 1$.
We need to find $p + q$:
$$p + q = 29 + 50 = 79$$

\boxed{79}
final_text for this step
The problem asks for the area of the region $\mathcal{T}$ on a disk of radius $R=200$ where a sphere of radius $r=42$ can be placed such that it sits on the disk and lies completely inside a hemisphere of radius $R=200$ centered at the same point.

**1. Geometric Setup:**
Let the center of the disk and the hemisphere be the origin $(0,0,0)$.
The hemisphere is defined by $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 \le R^2$ with $z \ge 0$.
The disk lies in the plane $z=0$ and is defined by $x^2 + y^2 \le R^2$.
Let $P = (x, y, 0)$ be a point on the disk where the small sphere is placed.
Since the small sphere of radius $r$ sits on the disk at $P$, its center $C$ must be at $(x, y, r)$.

**2. Condition for Containment:**
For the small sphere to lie completely inside the hemisphere, every point on the small sphere must satisfy the hemisphere's inequality.
The farthest point on the small sphere from the origin lies on the line segment connecting the origin to the center $C$, extended by the radius $r$.
The distance from the origin to the center $C$ is $d = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + r^2}$.
The distance from the origin to the farthest point on the small sphere is $d + r$.
The condition for the sphere to be inside the hemisphere is:
$$d + r \le R$$
Substituting $d$:
$$\sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + r^2} + r \le R$$

**3. Solving for the Region $\mathcal{T}$:**
Let $\rho = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ be the distance of point $P$ from the center of the disk.
The inequality becomes:
$$\sqrt{\rho^2 + r^2} \le R - r$$
Since $R = 200$ and $r = 42$, $R - r = 158 > 0$, so we can square both sides:
$$\rho^2 + r^2 \le (R - r)^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le (R - r)^2 - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le R^2 - 2Rr + r^2 - r^2$$
$$\rho^2 \le R^2 - 2Rr$$
$$\rho^2 \le R(R - 2r)$$

**4. Calculation:**
Given $R = 200$ and $r = 42$:
$$R - 2r = 200 - 2(42) = 200 - 84 = 116$$
$$\rho_{max}^2 = 200 \times 116 = 23200$$
The region $\mathcal{T}$ is a disk of radius $\rho_{max} = \sqrt{23200}$.
The area of $\mathcal{T}$ is:
$$\text{Area}(\mathcal{T}) = \pi \rho_{max}^2 = 23200\pi$$
The area of the original disk is:
$$\text{Area}(\text{Disk}) = \pi R^2 = \pi (200)^2 = 40000\pi$$

**5. Ratio and Final Answer:**
The ratio of the areas is:
$$\frac{\text{Area}(\mathcal{T})}{\text{Area}(\text{Disk})} = \frac{23200\pi}{40000\pi} = \frac{23200}{40000} = \frac{232}{400}$$
Simplify the fraction by dividing numerator and denominator by their greatest common divisor.
Both are divisible by 8:
$$232 \div 8 = 29$$
$$400 \div 8 = 50$$
So the ratio is $\frac{29}{50}$.
Here $p = 29$ and $q = 50$.
Check if they are relatively prime: $29$ is a prime number and does not divide $50$. Thus, $\gcd(29, 50) = 1$.
We need to find $p + q$:
$$p + q = 29 + 50 = 79$$

<answer>\boxed{79}</answer>

$$p + q = 29 + 50 = 79$$
